\section{Hoare: Hoare Logic, Part I}

In the final chaper of \textit{Logical Foundations} (\textit{Software
    Foundations}, volume 1), we began applying the mathematical tools
    developed in the first part of the course to studying the theory
    of a small programming language, Imp.



\begin{itemize}
\item  We defined a type of \textit{abstract syntax trees} for Imp, together
      with an \textit{evaluation relation} (a partial function on states)
      that specifies the \textit{operational semantics} of programs.


      The language we defined, though small, captures some of the key
      features of full-blown languages like C, C++, and Java,
      including the fundamental notion of mutable state and some
      common control structures.



\item  We proved a number of \textit{metatheoretic properties} -- ``meta'' in
      the sense that they are properties of the language as a whole,
      rather than of particular programs in the language.  These
      included:



\begin{itemize}
\item  determinism of evaluation



\item  equivalence of some different ways of writing down the
          definitions (e.g., functional and relational definitions of
          arithmetic expression evaluation)



\item  guaranteed termination of certain classes of programs



\item  correctness (in the sense of preserving meaning) of a number
          of useful program transformations



\item  behavioral equivalence of programs (in the \coqdoclibrary{Equiv}
          chapter). 
\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}


 If we stopped here, we would already have something useful: a set
    of tools for defining and discussing programming languages and
    language features that are mathematically precise, flexible, and
    easy to work with, applied to a set of key properties.  All of
    these properties are things that language designers, compiler
    writers, and users might care about knowing.  Indeed, many of them
    are so fundamental to our understanding of the programming
    languages we deal with that we might not consciously recognize
    them as ``theorems.''  But properties that seem intuitively obvious
    can sometimes be quite subtle (sometimes also subtly wrong!).


    We'll return to the theme of metatheoretic properties of whole
    languages later in this volume when we discuss \textit{types} and \textit{type
    soundness}.  In this chapter, though, we turn to a different set
    of issues.
 Our goal in this chapter is to develop the tools to work through
    some simple examples of \textit{program verification} -- i.e., to use the
    precise definition of Imp to prove formally that particular
    programs satisfy particular specifications of their behavior.


    We'll develop a reasoning system called \textit{Floyd-Hoare Logic} --
    often shortened to just \textit{Hoare Logic} -- in which each of the
    syntactic constructs of Imp is equipped with a generic ``proof
    rule'' that can be used to reason compositionally about the
    correctness of programs involving this construct. 

 Hoare Logic originated in the 1960s, and it continues to be the
    subject of intensive research right up to the present day.  It
    lies at the core of a multitude of tools that are being used in
    academia and industry to specify and verify real software systems. 

 Hoare Logic combines two beautiful ideas: a natural way of writing
    down \textit{specifications} of programs, and a \textit{structured proof
    technique} for proving that programs are correct with respect to
    such specifications -- where by ``structured'' we mean that the
    structure of proofs directly mirrors the structure of the programs
    that they are about.

\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\section{Assertions}



 An \textit{assertion} is a logical claim about the state of a program's
    memory -- formally, a property of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.state}{\coqdocdefinition{state}}s. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.Assertion}{Assertion}{\coqdocdefinition{Assertion}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.state}{\coqdocdefinition{state}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Prop}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
For example,



\begin{itemize}
\item  \coqdockw{fun} \coqdocvar{st} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqdocvar{st} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 3 holds for states \coqdocvar{st} in which value of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}
      is 3,



\item  \coqdockw{fun} \coqdocvar{st} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqexternalref{True}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocinductive{True}} hold for all states, and



\item  \coqdockw{fun} \coqdocvar{st} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqexternalref{False}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocinductive{False}} holds for no states. 
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Notations for Assertions}



 This way of writing assertions can be a little bit heavy,
    for two reasons: (1) every single assertion that we ever write is
    going to begin with \coqdockw{fun} \coqdocvar{st} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} ; and (2) this state \coqdocvar{st} is the
    only one that we ever use to look up variables in assertions (we
    will almost never need to talk about two different memory states at the
    same time).  For discussing examples informally, we'll adopt some
    simplifying conventions: we'll drop the initial \coqdockw{fun} \coqdocvar{st} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow}, and
    we'll write just \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} to mean \coqdocvar{st} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}.  Thus, instead of writing


      fun st => st X = m


    we'll write just


      \footnote{\url{ X = m }}.


 Here the ``doubly curly'' braces \{\{ and \}\} delimit
    the scope of an assertion.  We'll see more examples below. 

 This example also illustrates a convention that we'll use
    throughout the Hoare Logic chapters: in informal assertions,
    capital letters like \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}, and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Z}{\coqdocdefinition{Z}} are Imp variables, while
    lowercase letters like \coqdocvar{x}, \coqdocvar{y}, \coqdocvar{m}, and \coqdocvar{n} are ordinary Rocq
    variables (of type \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}).  This is why, when translating from
    informal to formal, we replace \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} with \coqdocvar{st} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} but leave \coqdocvar{m}
    alone.


    The convention described above can be implemented in Rocq with a
    little syntax magic, using coercions and annotation scopes, much
    as we did with the <\{ \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.com}{\coqdocinductive{com}} \}> notation in \coqdoclibrary{Imp}. This new
    notation automatically lifts \coqref{SECF.Imp.aexp}{\coqdocinductive{aexp}}s, numbers, and \coqdockw{Prop}s into
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Assertion}{\coqdocdefinition{Assertion}}s when they appear in the \{\{ \coqdocvar{\_} \}\} scope, or when Rocq
    knows that the type of an expression is \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Assertion}{\coqdocdefinition{Assertion}}.


    There is no need to understand the details of how these notation
    hacks work, so we hide them in the HTML version of the notes.  (We
    barely understand some of it ourselves!)  For the gory details,
    see the Rocq development.  
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.Aexp}{Aexp}{\coqdocdefinition{Aexp}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Type}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.state}{\coqdocdefinition{state}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent

One small limitation of this approach is that we don't have
    an automatic way to coerce a function application that appears
    within an assertion to make appropriate use of the state when its
    arguments should be interpets as Imp arithmetic expressions.
    Instead, we introduce a notation \#\coqdocvar{f} \coqdocvar{e1} .. \coqdocvar{en} that stands for (\coqdockw{fun}
    \coqdocvar{st} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqdocvar{f} (\coqdocvar{e1} \coqdocvar{st}) .. (\coqdocvar{en} \coqdocvar{st}), letting us manually mark such function
    calls when they're needed as part of an assertion.

Occasionally we need to ``escape'' a raw ``Rocq-defined'' function to express
    a particularly complicated assertion.  We can do that using a \$ prefix,
    as in \{\{ \$(\coqdocvar{raw\_rocq}) \}\}.


    For example, \{\{ \$(\coqdockw{fun} \coqdocvar{st} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqdockw{\ensuremath{\forall}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, \coqdocvar{st} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 0) \}\} indicates an assertion that
    every variable of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} maps to 0 in the given state.

    new notation.

\subsection{Assertion Implication}



 Given two assertions \coqdocvar{P} and \coqdocvar{Q}, we say that \coqdocvar{P} \textit{implies} \coqdocvar{Q},
    written \coqdocvar{P} ->> \coqdocvar{Q}, if, whenever \coqdocvar{P} holds in some state \coqdocvar{st}, \coqdocvar{Q}
    also holds. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.assert implies}{assert\_implies}{\coqdocdefinition{assert\_implies}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqdef{SECF.Hoare.P:26}{P}{\coqdocbinder{P}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.Q:27}{Q}{\coqdocbinder{Q}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Assertion}{\coqdocdefinition{Assertion}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Prop}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\forall}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.st:28}{st}{\coqdocbinder{st}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.P:26}{\coqdocvariable{P}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.st:28}{\coqdocvariable{st}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Q:27}{\coqdocvariable{Q}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.st:28}{\coqdocvariable{st}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Note that the notation for \textit{assertion implication} is analogous
    to the ``usual'' Rocq implication \ensuremath{\rightarrow}. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Notation} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:x '->>' x}{"}{"}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P ->> Q}}" \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.assert implies}{\coqdocdefinition{assert\_implies}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Q}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{12.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{at}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{level}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{80)}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{hoare\_spec\_scope}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
We'll also want the ``iff'' variant of implication between
    assertions: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Notation} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:x '<<->>' x}{"}{"}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P <<->> Q}}" \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:x '->>' x}{\coqdocnotation{->>}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Q}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '/x5C' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\land}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Q}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:x '->>' x}{\coqdocnotation{->>}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{P}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{13.00em}

\end{coqdoccode}
\section{Hoare Triples, Informally}



 A \textit{Hoare triple} is a claim about the state before and
    after executing a command.  The standard notation is


      \{P\} c \{Q\}


    meaning:



\begin{itemize}
\item  If command \coqdocvar{c} begins execution in a state satisfying assertion \coqdocvar{P},

\item  and if \coqdocvar{c} eventually terminates in some final state,

\item  then that final state will satisfy the assertion \coqdocvar{Q}.

\end{itemize}


    Assertion \coqdocvar{P} is called the \textit{precondition} of the triple, and \coqdocvar{Q} is
    the \textit{postcondition}.


    Because single braces are already used for other things in Rocq, we'll write
    Hoare triples with double braces:


       \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{Q}}
 For example,



\begin{itemize}
\item  The Hoare triple


          \footnote{\url{X = 0}} X := X + 1 \footnote{\url{X = 1}}


      states that command \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} := \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} + 1 will transform a state in
      which \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 0 to a state in which \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 1.



\item  On the other hand,


          forall m, \footnote{\url{X = m}} X := X + 1 \footnote{\url{X = m + 1}}


      is a \textit{proposition} stating that the Hoare triple \{\{\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m}\}\} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} :=
      \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} + 1 \{\{\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = \coqdocvar{m} + 1\}\} is valid for any choice of \coqdocvar{m}.  Note that
      \coqdocvar{m} in the two assertions is a reference to the \textit{Rocq} variable
      \coqdocvar{m}, which is bound outside the Hoare triple. 
\end{itemize}


\paragraph{Exercise: 1 star, standard, optional (triples)}




    Paraphrase the following in English.


     1) \footnote{\url{True}} c \footnote{\url{X = 5}}


     2) forall m, \footnote{\url{X = m}} c \footnote{\url{X = m + 5)}}


     3) \footnote{\url{X <= Y}} c \footnote{\url{Y <= X}}


     4) \footnote{\url{True}} c \footnote{\url{False}}


     5) forall m,
          \footnote{\url{X = m}}
          c
          \footnote{\url{Y = real_fact m}}


     6) forall m,
          \footnote{\url{X = m}}
          c
          \footnote{\url{(Z * Z) <= m /\ ~ (((S Z) * (S Z)) <= m)}}

\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\paragraph{Exercise: 1 star, standard, optional (valid\_triples)}




    Which of the following Hoare triples are \textit{valid} -- i.e., the
    claimed relation between \coqdocvar{P}, \coqdocvar{c}, and \coqdocvar{Q} is true?


   1) \footnote{\url{True}} X := 5 \footnote{\url{X = 5}}


   2) \footnote{\url{X = 2}} X := X + 1 \footnote{\url{X = 3}}


   3) \footnote{\url{True}} X := 5; Y := 0 \footnote{\url{X = 5}}


   4) \footnote{\url{X = 2 /\ X = 3}} X := 5 \footnote{\url{X = 0}}


   5) \footnote{\url{True}} skip \footnote{\url{False}}


   6) \footnote{\url{False}} skip \footnote{\url{True}}


   7) \footnote{\url{True}} while true do skip end \footnote{\url{False}}


   8) \footnote{\url{X = 0}}
        while X = 0 do X := X + 1 end
      \footnote{\url{X = 1}}


   9) \footnote{\url{X = 1}}
        while X <> 0 do X := X + 1 end
      \footnote{\url{X = 100}}

\section{Proof Rules}



 The goal of Hoare logic is to provide a \textit{compositional}
    method for proving the validity of specific Hoare triples.  That
    is, we want the structure of a program's correctness proof to
    mirror the structure of the program itself.  To this end, in the
    sections below, we'll introduce a rule for reasoning about each of
    the different syntactic forms of commands in Imp -- one for
    assignment, one for sequencing, one for conditionals, etc. -- plus
    a couple of ``structural'' rules for gluing things together.  We
    will then be able to prove programs correct using these proof
    rules, without ever unfolding the definition of \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.valid hoare triple}{\coqdocdefinition{valid\_hoare\_triple}}. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Skip}



 Since \coqdocvar{skip} doesn't change the state, it preserves any
    assertion \coqdocvar{P}:
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}  (hoare\_skip)
      \footnote{\url{ P }} skip \footnote{\url{ P }}

\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Theorem} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.hoare skip}{hoare\_skip}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_skip}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\forall}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.P:42}{P}{\coqdocbinder{P}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{2.50em}

\subsection{Sequencing}



 If command \coqdocvar{c1} takes any state where \coqdocvar{P} holds to a state where
    \coqdocvar{Q} holds, and if \coqdocvar{c2} takes any state where \coqdocvar{Q} holds to one
    where \coqdocvar{R} holds, then doing \coqdocvar{c1} followed by \coqdocvar{c2} will take any
    state where \coqdocvar{P} holds to one where \coqdocvar{R} holds:


        \footnote{\url{ P }} c1 \footnote{\url{ Q }}
        \footnote{\url{ Q }} c2 \footnote{\url{ R }}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}  (hoare\_seq)
       \footnote{\url{ P }} c1;c2 \footnote{\url{ R }}

Note that, in the formal rule \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare seq}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_seq}}, the premises are
    given in backwards order (\coqdocvar{c2} before \coqdocvar{c1}).  This matches the
    natural flow of information in many of the situations where we'll
    use the rule, since the natural way to construct a Hoare-logic
    proof is to begin at the end of the program (with the final
    postcondition) and push postconditions backwards through commands
    until we reach the beginning. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Assignment}



 The rule for assignment is the most fundamental of the Hoare
    logic proof rules.  Here's how it works.


    Consider this incomplete Hoare triple:


       \footnote{\url{ ??? }}  X := Y  \footnote{\url{ X = 1 }}


    We want to assign \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} to \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and finish in a state where \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} is 1.
    What could the precondition be?


    One possibility is \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = 1, because if \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} is already 1 then
    assigning it to \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} causes \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} to be 1.  That leads to a valid
    Hoare triple:


       \footnote{\url{ Y = 1 }}  X := Y  \footnote{\url{ X = 1 }}


    It may seem as though coming up with that precondition must have
    taken some clever thought.  But there is a mechanical way we could
    have done it: if we take the postcondition \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 1 and in it
    replace \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} with \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}---that is, replace the left-hand side of the
    assignment statement with the right-hand side---we get the
    precondition, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = 1. 

 That same idea works in more complicated cases.  For
    example:


       \footnote{\url{ ??? }}  X := X + Y  \footnote{\url{ X = 1 }}


    If we replace the \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} in \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 1 with \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} + \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}, we get \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} + \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = 1.
    That again leads to a valid Hoare triple:


       \footnote{\url{ X + Y = 1 }}  X := X + Y  \footnote{\url{ X = 1 }}


    Why does this technique work?  The postcondition identifies some
    property \coqdocvar{P} that we want to hold of the variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} being
    assigned.  In this case, \coqdocvar{P} is ``equals 1''.  To complete the
    triple and make it valid, we need to identify a precondition that
    guarantees that property will hold of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}.  Such a precondition
    must ensure that the same property holds of \textit{whatever is being
    assigned to} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}.  So, in the example, we need ``equals 1'' to
    hold of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} + \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}.  That's exactly what the technique guarantees. 

 In general, the postcondition could be some arbitrary assertion
    \coqdocvar{Q}, and the right-hand side of the assignment could be some
    arbitrary arithmetic expression \coqdocvar{a}:


       \footnote{\url{ ??? }}  X := a  \footnote{\url{ Q }}


    The precondition would then be \coqdocvar{Q}, but with any occurrences of
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} in it replaced by \coqdocvar{a}.


    Let's introduce a notation for this idea of replacing occurrences:
    Define \coqdocvar{Q} [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} |-> \coqdocvar{a}] to mean ``\coqdocvar{Q} where \coqdocvar{a} is substituted in
    place of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}''.


    This yields the Hoare logic rule for assignment:


      \footnote{\url{ Q [X |-> a] }}  X := a  \footnote{\url{ Q }}


    One way of reading this rule is: If you want statement \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} := \coqdocvar{a}
    to terminate in a state that satisfies assertion \coqdocvar{Q}, then it
    suffices to start in a state that also satisfies \coqdocvar{Q}, except
    where \coqdocvar{a} is substituted for every occurrence of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}. 

 To many people, this rule seems ``backwards'' at first, because
    it proceeds from the postcondition to the precondition.  Actually
    it makes good sense to go in this direction: the postcondition is
    often what is more important, because it characterizes what will be
    true after running the code.


    Nonetheless, it's also possible to formulate a ``forward'' assignment
    rule.  We'll do that later in some exercises. 

 Here are some valid instances of the assignment rule:


      \footnote{\url{ (X <= 5) [X |-> X + 1] }}         (that is, X + 1 <= 5)
        X := X + 1
      \footnote{\url{ X <= 5 }}


      \footnote{\url{ (X = 3) [X |-> 3] }}              (that is, 3 = 3)
        X := 3
      \footnote{\url{ X = 3 }}


      \footnote{\url{ (0 <= X /\ X <= 5) [X |-> 3] }}.  (that is, 0 <= 3 /\symbol{92} 3 <= 5)
        X := 3
      \footnote{\url{ 0 <= X /\ X <= 5 }}


 To formalize the rule, we must first formalize the idea of
    ``substituting an expression for an Imp variable in an assertion'',
    which we refer to as assertion substitution, or \coqref{SECF.Hoare.assertion sub}{\coqdocdefinition{assertion\_sub}}.


    Intuitively, given a proposition \coqdocvar{P}, a variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, and an
    arithmetic expression \coqdocvar{a}, we want to derive another proposition
    \coqdocvar{P'} that is just the same as \coqdocvar{P} except that \coqdocvar{P'} should mention
    \coqdocvar{a} wherever \coqdocvar{P} mentions \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}. 

 This operation is related to the idea of substituting Imp
    expressions for Imp variables that we saw in \coqdoclibrary{Equiv}
    (\coqref{SECF.Equiv.subst aexp}{\coqdocdefinition{subst\_aexp}} and friends). The difference is that, here,
    \coqdocvar{P} is an arbitrary Rocq assertion, so we can't directly
    ``edit'' its text. 

 However, we can achieve the same effect by evaluating \coqdocvar{P} in an
    updated state, defined as follows:

 This notation allows us to write this operation as:


        P \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} |-> \coqdocvar{a} 


 That is, \coqdocvar{P} [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} |-> \coqdocvar{a}] stands for an assertion -- let's call it
    \coqdocvar{P'} -- that behaves just like \coqdocvar{P} except that, wherever \coqdocvar{P} looks up
    the variable \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} in the current state, \coqdocvar{P'} instead uses the value
    of the expression \coqdocvar{a}. 

 To see how this works in more detail, let's calculate what happens with
    a couple of examples.  First, suppose \coqdocvar{P'} is (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} 5) [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} |-> 3] --
    that is, more formally, \coqdocvar{P'} is the Rocq expression


    fun st =>
      (fun st' => st' X <= 5)
      (X !-> aeval st 3 ; st),


    which simplifies to


    fun st =>
      (fun st' => st' X <= 5)
      (X !-> 3 ; st)


    and further simplifies to


    fun st =>
      ((X !-> 3 ; st) X) <= 5


    and finally to


    fun st =>
      3 <= 5.


    That is, \coqdocvar{P'} is the assertion that 3 is less than or equal to
    5 (as expected). 

 For a more interesting example, suppose \coqdocvar{P'} is (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \ensuremath{\le} 5) [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} |->
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} + 1].  Formally, \coqdocvar{P'} is the Rocq expression


    fun st =>
      (fun st' => st' X <= 5)
      (X !-> aeval st (X + 1); st),


    which simplifies to


    fun st =>
      (X !-> aeval st (X + 1) ; st) X <= 5


    and further simplifies to


    fun st =>
      (aeval st (X + 1)) <= 5.


    That is, \coqdocvar{P'} is the assertion that \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} + 1 is at most 5.


 We can demonstrate formally that we have captured intuitive meaning of
    ``assertion subsitution'' by proving some example logical equivalences:

Now, using the substitution operation we've just defined, we can
    give the precise proof rule for assignment:
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (hoare\_asgn)
      \footnote{\url{Q [X |-> a]}} X := a \footnote{\url{Q}}


 We can prove formally that this rule is indeed valid.

\coqdocindent{1.00em}

\coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:x ':=' x}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com::x '+' x}{\coqdocnotation{+}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{1}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '<' x}{\coqdocnotation{<}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{5}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}}.\coqdoceol

Of course, we'd probably prefer to work with this simpler triple:


      \footnote{\url{X < 4}} X := X + 1 \footnote{\url{X < 5}}


   We will see how to do so in the next section. 

 Complete these Hoare triples by providing an appropriate
    precondition using \coqdoctac{\ensuremath{\exists}}, then prove then with \coqdoctac{apply}
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare asgn}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_asgn}}. If you find that tactic doesn't suffice, double check
    that you have completed the triple properly.

\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocnoindent

\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, standard, especially useful (hoare\_asgn\_wrong)}



 The assignment rule looks backward to almost everyone the first
    time they see it.  If it still seems puzzling to you, it may help
    to think a little about alternative ``forward'' rules.  Here is a
    seemingly natural one:
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (hoare\_asgn\_wrong)
      \footnote{\url{ True }} X := a \footnote{\url{ X = a }}


    Give a counterexample showing that this rule is incorrect and use
    it to complete the proof below, showing that it is really a
    counterexample.  (Hint: The rule universally quantifies over the
    arithmetic expression \coqdocvar{a}, so your counterexample needs to
    exhibit an \coqdocvar{a} for which the rule doesn't work.) 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Theorem} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.hoare asgn wrong}{hoare\_asgn\_wrong}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_asgn\_wrong}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:'exists' x '..' x ',' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\exists}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.a:57}{a}{\coqdocbinder{a}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}}\coqref{SECF.Imp.aexp}{\coqdocinductive{aexp}}\coqexternalref{::type scope:'exists' x '..' x ',' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{,}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqexternalref{::type scope:'x7E' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\lnot}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:'True'}{\coqdocnotation{True}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:x ':=' x}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.a:57}{\coqdocvariable{a}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '=' x}{\coqdocnotation{=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.a:57}{\coqdocvariable{a}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Proof}}.\coqdoceol
 \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Admitted}}.\coqdoceol

\end{coqdoccode}
\paragraph{Exercise: 3 stars, advanced, optional (hoare\_asgn\_fwd)}




    By using a \textit{parameter} \coqdocvar{m} (a Rocq number) to remember the
    original value of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} we can define a Hoare rule for assignment
    that does, intuitively, ``work forwards'' rather than backwards.
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (hoare\_asgn\_fwd)
       \footnote{\url{fun st => P st /\ st X = m}}
         X := a
       \footnote{\url{fun st => P (X !-> m ; st) /\ st X = aeval (X !-> m ; st) a }}


    Note that we need to write out the postcondition in ``desugared''
    form, because it needs to talk about two different states: we use
    the original value of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} to reconstruct the state \coqdocvar{st'} before the
    assignment took place.  (Also note that this rule is more complicated
    than \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare asgn}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_asgn}}!)


    Prove that this rule is correct.

\coqdocnoindent

\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, advanced, optional (hoare\_asgn\_fwd\_exists)}




    Another way to define a forward rule for assignment is to
    existentially quantify over the previous value of the assigned
    variable.  Prove that it is correct.
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (hoare\_asgn\_fwd\_exists)
      \footnote{\url{fun st => P st}}
        X := a
      \footnote{\url{fun st => exists m, P (X !-> m ; st) /\
                     st X = aeval (X !-> m ; st) a }}

\subsection{Consequence}



 Sometimes the preconditions and postconditions we get from the
    Hoare rules won't quite be the ones we want in the particular
    situation at hand -- they may be logically equivalent but have a
    different syntactic form that fails to unify with the goal we are
    trying to prove, or they actually may be logically weaker (for
    preconditions) or stronger (for postconditions) than what we need. 

 For instance,


      \footnote{\url{(X = 3) [X |-> 3]}} X := 3 \footnote{\url{X = 3}},


    follows directly from the assignment rule, but


      \footnote{\url{True}} X := 3 \footnote{\url{X = 3}}


    does not.  This triple is valid, but it is not an instance of
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare asgn}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_asgn}} because \coqexternalref{True}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocinductive{True}} and (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 3) [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} |-> 3] are not
    syntactically equal assertions.


    However, they are logically \textit{equivalent}, so if one triple is
    valid, then the other must certainly be as well.  We can capture
    this observation with the following rule:


                \footnote{\url{P'}} c \footnote{\url{Q}}
                  P \texttt{-} P'
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}
                \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{Q}}


 Taking this line of thought a bit further, we can see that
    strengthening the precondition or weakening the postcondition of a
    valid triple always produces another valid triple. This
    observation is captured by two \textit{Rules of Consequence}.


                \footnote{\url{P'}} c \footnote{\url{Q}}
                   P ->> P'
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (hoare\_consequence\_pre)
                \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{Q}}


                \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{Q'}}
                  Q' ->> Q
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}    (hoare\_consequence\_post)

For example, we can use the first consequence rule like this:


    \footnote{\url{ True }} ->>
    \footnote{\url{ (X = 1) [X |-> 1] }}
      X := 1
    \footnote{\url{ X = 1 }}


    Or, formally...

We can also use it to prove the example mentioned earlier.


    \footnote{\url{ X < 4 }} ->>
    \footnote{\url{ (X < 5)[X |-> X + 1] }}
      X := X + 1
    \footnote{\url{ X < 5 }}

Finally, here is a combined rule of consequence that allows us to
    vary both the precondition and the postcondition.


                \footnote{\url{P'}} c \footnote{\url{Q'}}
                   P ->> P'
                   Q' ->> Q
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (hoare\_consequence)
                \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{Q}}

\subsection{Automation}



 Many of the proofs we have done so far with Hoare triples can be
    streamlined using the automation techniques that we introduced in
    the \coqdocvar{Auto} chapter of \textit{Logical Foundations}.


    Recall that the \coqdoctac{auto} tactic can be told to \coqdoctac{unfold} definitions
    as part of its proof search.  Let's give it that hint for the
    definitions and coercions we're using: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Hint Unfold} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.assert implies}{\coqdocdefinition{assert\_implies}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.assertion sub}{\coqdocdefinition{assertion\_sub}} \coqref{SECF.Maps.t update}{\coqdocdefinition{t\_update}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{core}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Hint Unfold} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.valid hoare triple}{\coqdocdefinition{valid\_hoare\_triple}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{core}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Hint Unfold} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.assert of Prop}{\coqdocdefinition{assert\_of\_Prop}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Aexp of nat}{\coqdocdefinition{Aexp\_of\_nat}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Aexp of aexp}{\coqdocdefinition{Aexp\_of\_aexp}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{core}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Also recall that \coqdoctac{auto} will search for a proof involving \coqdoctac{intros}
    and \coqdoctac{apply}.  By default, the theorems that it will apply include
    any of the local hypotheses, as well as theorems in the ``core'' hint
    database. 

 The proof of \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare consequence pre}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_consequence\_pre}}, repeated below, looks
    like an opportune place for such automation, because all it does
    is \coqdoctac{unfold}, \coqdoctac{intros}, and \coqdoctac{apply}.  (It uses \coqdoctac{assumption}, too,
    but that's just application of a hypothesis.)

Merely using \coqdoctac{auto}, though, doesn't complete the proof.

\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Abort}}.\coqdoceol

The problem is the \coqdoctac{apply} \coqdocvar{Hhoare} \coqdockw{with}... part of the proof.  Rocq
    isn't able to figure out how to instantiate \coqdocvar{st} without some help
    from us.  Recall, though, that there are versions of many tactics
    that will use \textit{existential variables} to make progress even when
    the regular versions of those tactics would get stuck.


    Here, the \coqdoctac{eapply} tactic will introduce an existential variable
    ?\coqdocvar{st} as a placeholder for \coqdocvar{st}, and \coqdocvar{eassumption} will
    instantiate ?\coqdocvar{st} with \coqdocvar{st} when it discovers \coqdocvar{st} in assumption
    \coqdocvar{Heval}.  By using \coqdoctac{eapply} we are essentially telling Rocq, ``Be
    patient: The missing part is going to be filled in later in the
    proof.'' 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent

The \coqdoctac{eauto} tactic will use \coqdoctac{eapply} as part of its proof search.
    So, the entire proof can actually be done in just one line.

Of course, it's hard to predict that \coqdoctac{eauto} suffices here
    without having gone through the original proof of
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare consequence pre}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_consequence\_pre}} to see the tactics it used. But now that
    we know \coqdoctac{eauto} worked there, it's a good bet that it will also
    work for \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare consequence post}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_consequence\_post}}.

The final bullet of that proof also looks like a candidate for
    automation. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Example} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.hoare asgn example1''}{hoare\_asgn\_example1'{}'}{\coqdocdefinition{hoare\_asgn\_example1'{}'}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:'True'}{\coqdocnotation{True}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:x ':=' x}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{1}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '=' x}{\coqdocnotation{=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{1}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Proof}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{eapply}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.hoare consequence pre}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_consequence\_pre}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{-}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{apply}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.hoare asgn}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_asgn}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{-}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{auto}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Qed}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Now we have quite a nice proof script: it simply identifies the
    Hoare rules that need to be used and leaves the remaining
    low-level details up to Rocq to figure out. 

 By now it might be apparent that the \textit{entire} proof could be
    automated if we added \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare consequence pre}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_consequence\_pre}} and \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare asgn}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_asgn}} to
    the hint database.  We won't do that in this chapter, so that we
    can get a better understanding of when and how the Hoare rules are
    used.  In the next chapter, \coqdoclibrary{Hoare2}, we'll dive deeper into
    automating entire proofs of Hoare triples. 

 The other example of using consequence that we did earlier,
    \coqdocvar{hoare\_asgn\_example2}, requires a little more work to automate.
    We can streamline the first line with \coqdoctac{eapply}, but we can't just use
    \coqdoctac{auto} for the final bullet, since it needs \coqdocvar{lia}.

Again, we have quite a nice proof script.  All the low-level
    details of proofs about assertions have been taken care of
    automatically. Of course, \coqdocvar{assertion\_auto} isn't able to prove
    everything we could possibly want to know about assertions --
    there's no magic here! But it's pretty good. 

\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, standard (hoare\_asgn\_examples\_2)}




    Prove these triples.  Try to make your proof scripts nicely
    automated by following the examples above.

\subsection{Sequencing + Assignment}



 Here's an example of a program involving both sequencing and
    assignment.  Note the use of \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare seq}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_seq}} in conjunction with
    \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare consequence pre}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_consequence\_pre}} and the \coqdoctac{eapply} tactic.

\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent

\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.a:99}{\coqdocvariable{a}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '=' x}{\coqdocnotation{=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.n:100}{\coqdocvariable{n}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{2.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:x ':=' x}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.a:99}{\coqdocvariable{a}}\coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:x ';' x}{\coqdocnotation{;}}\coqdoceol

\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Imp.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '=' x}{\coqdocnotation{=}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.n:100}{\coqdocvariable{n}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}}.\coqdoceol

Informally, a nice way of displaying a proof using the sequencing
    rule is as a ``decorated program'' where the intermediate assertion
    \coqdocvar{Q} is written between \coqdocvar{c1} and \coqdocvar{c2}:


              \footnote{\url{ a = n }}
     X := a
              \footnote{\url{ X = n }};    <--- decoration for Q
     skip
              \footnote{\url{ X = n }}
 We'll come back to the idea of decorated programs in much more
    detail in the next chapter. 

\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, standard, especially useful (hoare\_asgn\_example4)}




    Translate this ``decorated program'' into a formal proof:


                   \footnote{\url{ True }} ->>
                   \footnote{\url{ 1 = 1 }}
    X := 1
                   \footnote{\url{ X = 1 }} ->>
                   \footnote{\url{ X = 1 /\ 2 = 2 }};
    Y := 2
                   \footnote{\url{ X = 1 /\ Y = 2 }}


   Note the use of ``->>'' decorations, each marking a use of
   \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare consequence pre}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_consequence\_pre}}.


   We've started you off by providing a use of \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare seq}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_seq}} that
   explicitly identifies \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 1 as the intermediate assertion.

\paragraph{Exercise: 3 stars, standard (swap\_exercise)}




    Write an Imp program \coqdocvar{c} that swaps the values of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} and
    show that it satisfies the following specification:


      \footnote{\url{X <= Y}} c \footnote{\url{Y <= X}}


    Your proof should not need to use \coqdoctac{unfold} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.valid hoare triple}{\coqdocdefinition{valid\_hoare\_triple}}.


    Hints:

\begin{itemize}
\item  Remember that Imp commands need to be enclosed in <\{...\}>
         brackets.

\item  Remember that the assignment rule works best when it's
         applied ``back to front,'' from the postcondition to the
         precondition.  So your proof will want to start at the end
         and work back to the beginning of your program.

\item  Remember that \coqdoctac{eapply} is your friend.)  

\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Exercise: 4 stars, advanced (invalid\_triple)}




    Show that


    \footnote{\url{ a = n }} X := 3; Y := a \footnote{\url{ Y = n }}


    is not a valid Hoare triple for some choices of \coqdocvar{a} and \coqdocvar{n}.


    Conceptual hint: Invent a particular \coqdocvar{a} and \coqdocvar{n} for which the
    triple in invalid, then use those to complete the proof.


    Technical hint: Hypothesis \coqdocvar{H} below begins \coqdockw{\ensuremath{\forall}} \coqdocvar{a} \coqdocvar{n}, ....
    You'll want to instantiate that with the particular \coqdocvar{a} and \coqdocvar{n}
    you've invented.  You can do that with \coqdoctac{assert} and \coqdoctac{apply}, but
    you may remember (from \coqdocvar{Tactics.v} in Logical Foundations)
    that Rocq offers an even easier tactic: \coqdoctac{specialize}.  If you write


       specialize H with (a := your\_a) (n := your\_n)


    the hypothesis will be instantiated on \coqdocvar{your\_a} and \coqdocvar{your\_n}.


    Having chosen your \coqdocvar{a} and \coqdocvar{n}, proceed as follows:

\begin{itemize}
\item  Use the (assumed) validity of the given hoare triple to derive
       a state \coqdocvar{st'} in which \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} has some value \coqdocvar{y1}

\item  Use the evaluation rules (\coqref{SECF.Hoare.Himp.E Seq}{\coqdocconstructor{E\_Seq}} and \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.E Asgn}{\coqdocconstructor{E\_Asgn}}) to show that
       \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} has a \textit{different} value \coqdocvar{y2} in the same final state \coqdocvar{st'}

\item  Since \coqdocvar{y1} and \coqdocvar{y2} are both equal to \coqdocvar{st'} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}, they are equal
       to each other. But we chose them to be different, so this is a
       contradiction, which finishes the proof.

\end{itemize}

\coqdocnoindent

\subsection{Conditionals}



 What sort of rule do we want for reasoning about conditional
    commands?


    Certainly, if the same assertion \coqdocvar{Q} holds after executing
    either of the branches, then it holds after the whole conditional.
    So we might be tempted to write:


              \footnote{\url{P}} c1 \footnote{\url{Q}}
              \footnote{\url{P}} c2 \footnote{\url{Q}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}
      \footnote{\url{P}} if b then c1 else c2 \footnote{\url{Q}}


 However, this is rather weak. For example, using this rule,
   we cannot show


     \footnote{\url{ True }}
       if X = 0
         then Y := 2
         else Y := X + 1
       end
     \footnote{\url{ X <= Y }}


   since the rule doesn't tell us enough about the state in which the
   assignments take place in the ``then'' and ``else'' branches. 

 Fortunately, we can say something more precise.  In the
    ``then'' branch, we know that the boolean expression \coqdocvar{b} evaluates to
    \coqexternalref{true}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{true}}, and in the ``else'' branch, we know it evaluates to \coqexternalref{false}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{false}}.
    Making this information available in the premises of the rule gives
    us more information to work with when reasoning about the behavior
    of \coqdocvar{c1} and \coqdocvar{c2} (i.e., the reasons why they establish the
    postcondition \coqdocvar{Q}).


              \footnote{\url{P /\   b}} c1 \footnote{\url{Q}}
              \footnote{\url{P /\ ~ b}} c2 \footnote{\url{Q}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}  (hoare\_if)
      \footnote{\url{P}} if b then c1 else c2 end \footnote{\url{Q}}


 To interpret this rule formally, we need to do a little work.
    Strictly speaking, the assertion we've written, \coqdocvar{P} \ensuremath{\land} \coqdocvar{b}, is the
    conjunction of an assertion and a boolean expression -- i.e., it
    doesn't typecheck.  To fix this, we need a way of formally
    ``lifting'' any bexp \coqdocvar{b} to an assertion.  We'll write \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}} \coqdocvar{b} for
    the assertion ``the boolean expression \coqdocvar{b} evaluates to \coqexternalref{true}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{true}} (in
    the given state).'' 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.b:103}{b}{\coqdocbinder{b}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.Assertion}{\coqdocdefinition{Assertion}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{fun}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.st:104}{st}{\coqdocbinder{st}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\Rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqref{SECF.Imp.beval}{\coqdocdefinition{beval}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.st:104}{\coqdocvariable{st}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.b:103}{\coqdocvariable{b}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '=' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{=}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:::'true'}{\coqdocnotation{true}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Coercion} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bexp}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{>->}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{Assertion}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Arguments} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{/}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
A useful fact about \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}}: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Lemma} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.bexp eval false}{bexp\_eval\_false}{\coqdoclemma{bexp\_eval\_false}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\forall}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.b:105}{b}{\coqdocbinder{b}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.st:106}{st}{\coqdocbinder{st}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Imp.beval}{\coqdocdefinition{beval}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.st:106}{\coqdocvariable{st}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.b:105}{\coqdocvariable{b}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '=' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{=}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:::'false'}{\coqdocnotation{false}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:'x7E' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\lnot}}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:'x7E' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.b:105}{\coqdocvariable{b}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.st:106}{\coqdocvariable{st}}\coqexternalref{::type scope:'x7E' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{)}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Proof}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{congruence}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Qed}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Hint Resolve} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{bexp\_eval\_false}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{core}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
We mentioned the \coqdoctac{congruence} tactic in passing in
    \coqdocvar{Auto} when building the \coqdocvar{find\_rwd} tactic.  Like
    \coqdocvar{find\_rwd}, \coqdoctac{congruence} is able to automatically find that both
    \coqref{SECF.Imp.beval}{\coqdocdefinition{beval}} \coqdocvar{st} \coqdocvar{b} = \coqexternalref{false}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{false}} and \coqref{SECF.Imp.beval}{\coqdocdefinition{beval}} \coqdocvar{st} \coqdocvar{b} = \coqexternalref{true}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{true}} are being assumed,
    notice the contradiction, and \coqdoctac{discriminate} to complete the
    proof. 

 Now we can formalize the Hoare proof rule for conditionals
    and prove it correct.

\coqdocindent{1.00em}

\coqdocindent{1.00em}

\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.P:107}{\coqdocvariable{P}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:'if' x 'then' x 'else' x 'end'}{\coqdocnotation{if}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.b:109}{\coqdocvariable{b}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:'if' x 'then' x 'else' x 'end'}{\coqdocnotation{then}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.c1:110}{\coqdocvariable{c1}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:'if' x 'then' x 'else' x 'end'}{\coqdocnotation{else}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.c2:111}{\coqdocvariable{c2}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.:com:com scope:'if' x 'then' x 'else' x 'end'}{\coqdocnotation{end}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\{\{}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.Q:108}{\coqdocvariable{Q}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.::hoare spec scope:'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D' x 'x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}}}.\coqdoceol
\end{coqdoccode}
That is (unwrapping the notations):


      Theorem hoare\_if : forall P Q b c1 c2,
        \footnote{\url{fun st => P st /\ bassertion b st}} c1 \footnote{\url{Q}} ->
        \footnote{\url{fun st => P st /\ ~ (bassertion b st)}} c2 \footnote{\url{Q}} ->
        \footnote{\url{P}} if b then c1 else c2 end \footnote{\url{Q}}.

\begin{coqdoccode}

\subsubsection{Example}



 Here is a formal proof that the program we used to motivate
    the rule satisfies the specification we wanted.

As we did earlier, it would be nice to eliminate all the low-level
    proof script that isn't about the Hoare rules.  Unfortunately, the
    \coqdocvar{assertion\_auto} tactic we wrote wasn't quite up to the job.  Looking
    at the proof of \coqref{SECF.Hoare.if example}{\coqdocdefinition{if\_example}}, we can see why.  We had to unfold a
    definition (\coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}}) and use a theorem (\coqdoclemma{eqb\_eq}) that we didn't
    need in earlier proofs.  So, let's add those into our tactic, and
    clean it up a little in the process. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Ltac} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{assertion\_auto'}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{unfold}} "->>"\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.assertion sub}{\coqdocdefinition{assertion\_sub}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}} \coqref{SECF.Maps.t update}{\coqdocdefinition{t\_update}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{,}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.bassertion}{\coqdocdefinition{bassertion}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{;}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{intros}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{;}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{simpl}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{in}} *\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{;}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{try}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{rewrite}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqdoclemma{eqb\_eq} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{in}} *\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{;}} \coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{auto}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{;}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{try}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{lia}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Now the proof is quite streamlined. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent

\subsubsection{Exercise: One-sided conditionals}



 In this exercise we consider extending Imp with ``one-sided
    conditionals'' of the form \coqdocvar{if1} \coqdocvar{b} \coqdockw{then} \coqdocvar{c} \coqdockw{end}. Here \coqdocvar{b} is a boolean
    expression, and \coqdocvar{c} is a command. If \coqdocvar{b} evaluates to \coqexternalref{true}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{true}}, then
    command \coqdocvar{c} is evaluated. If \coqdocvar{b} evaluates to \coqexternalref{false}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{false}}, then \coqdocvar{if1} \coqdocvar{b}
    \coqdockw{then} \coqdocvar{c} \coqdockw{end} does nothing.


    We recommend that you complete this exercise before attempting the
    ones that follow, as it should help solidify your understanding of
    the material. 

 The first step is to extend the syntax of commands and introduce
    the usual notations.  (We've done this for you, in a separate
    module to prevent polluting the global name space.) 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Module} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.If1}{If1}{\coqdocmodule{If1}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Inductive} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.If1.com}{com}{\coqdocinductive{com}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Type}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}

\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{|}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.If1.CAsgn}{CAsgn}{\coqdocconstructor{CAsgn}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqdocinductive{string} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.aexp}{\coqdocinductive{aexp}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.com:112}{\coqdocinductive{com}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{|}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.If1.CSeq}{CSeq}{\coqdocconstructor{CSeq}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.com:112}{\coqdocinductive{com}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.com:112}{\coqdocinductive{com}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.com:112}{\coqdocinductive{com}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{|}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.If1.CIf}{CIf}{\coqdocconstructor{CIf}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.bexp}{\coqdocinductive{bexp}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.com:112}{\coqdocinductive{com}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.com:112}{\coqdocinductive{com}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.com:112}{\coqdocinductive{com}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}

\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, standard (hoare\_if1)}



 Invent a Hoare logic proof rule for \coqdocvar{if1}.  State and prove a
    theorem named \coqdocvar{hoare\_if1} that shows the validity of your rule.
    Use \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare if}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_if}} as a guide. Try to invent a rule that is
    \textit{complete}, meaning it can be used to prove the correctness of as
    many one-sided conditionals as possible.  Also try to keep your
    rule \textit{compositional}, meaning that any Imp command that appears
    in a premise should syntactically be a part of the command
    in the conclusion.


    Hint: if you encounter difficulty getting Rocq to parse part of
    your rule as an assertion, try manually indicating that it should
    be in the assertion scope.  For example, if you want \coqdocvar{e} to be
    parsed as an assertion, write it as (\coqdocvar{e})\%\coqdocvar{assertion}. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
For example (\coqref{SECF.Hoare.If1.hoare if1 good}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_if1\_good}}) your rule should be strong
    enough to show the following Hoare triple is valid:


  \footnote{\url{ X + Y = Z }}
    if1 Y <> 0 then
      X := X + Y
    end
  \footnote{\url{ X = Z }}

\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.If1.manual grade for hoare if1}{manual\_grade\_for\_hoare\_if1}{\coqdocdefinition{manual\_grade\_for\_hoare\_if1}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqexternalref{option}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{option}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{(}}\coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}\coqexternalref{::type scope:x '*' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\times}}}\coqdocinductive{string}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{)}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}} \coqexternalref{None}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{None}}.\coqdoceol
\end{coqdoccode}
\ensuremath{\Box}

\paragraph{Exercise: 2 stars, standard (hoare\_if1\_good)}



 Use your \coqdocvar{if1} rule to prove the following (valid) Hoare triple.


    Hint: \coqdocvar{assertion\_auto'{}'} will once again get you most but not all
    the way to a completely automated proof.  You can finish manually,
    or tweak the tactic further.


    Hint: If you see a message like \coqdocvar{Unable} \coqdocvar{to} \coqdocvar{unify} "\coqdocvar{Imp.ceval}
    \coqdocvar{Imp.CSkip} \coqdocvar{st} \coqdocvar{st'}" \coqdockw{with}..., it probably means you are using a
    definition or theorem \coqdocvar{e.g}., \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.hoare skip}{\coqdoclemma{hoare\_skip}} from above this exercise
    without re-proving it for the new version of Imp with if1. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent

\subsection{While Loops}



 The Hoare rule for \coqdocvar{while} loops is based on the idea of a
    \textit{command invariant} (or just \textit{invariant}): an assertion whose
    truth is guaranteed after executing a command, assuming it is true
    before.


    That is, an assertion \coqdocvar{P} is a command invariant of \coqdocvar{c} if


      \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{P}}


    holds.  Note that the command invariant might temporarily become
    false in the middle of executing \coqdocvar{c}, but by the end of \coqdocvar{c} it
    must be restored. 

  As a first attempt at a \coqdocvar{while} rule, we could try:


             \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{P}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}
       while b do c end {{P\footnote{\url{P}}\}


    This rule is valid: if \coqdocvar{P} is a command invariant of \coqdocvar{c}, as the
    premise requires, then, no matter how many times the loop body
    executes, \coqdocvar{P} is going to be true when the loop finally finishes.


    But the rule also omits two crucial pieces of information.  First,
    the loop terminates when \coqdocvar{b} becomes false.  So we can strengthen
    the postcondition in the conclusion:


              \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{P}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}
       while b do c end {{P /\ ~b\footnote{\url{P}}\}


    Second, the loop body will be executed only if \coqdocvar{b} is true.  So we
    can also strengthen the precondition in the premise:


            \footnote{\url{P /\ b}} c \footnote{\url{P}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (hoare\_while)
       while b do c end {{P /\ ~b\footnote{\url{P}}\}


 That is the Hoare \coqdocvar{while} rule.  Note how it combines
    aspects of \coqdocvar{skip} and conditionals:



\begin{itemize}
\item  If the loop body executes zero times, the rule is like \coqdocvar{skip} in
      that the precondition survives to become (part of) the
      postcondition.



\item  Like a conditional, we can assume guard \coqdocvar{b} holds on entry to
      the subcommand. 

\end{itemize}

We call \coqdocvar{P} a \textit{loop invariant} of \coqdocvar{while} \coqdocvar{b} \coqdoctac{do} \coqdocvar{c} \coqdockw{end} if


      \footnote{\url{P /\ b}} c \footnote{\url{P}}


    is a valid Hoare triple.


    This means that \coqdocvar{P} will be true at the end of the loop body
    whenever the loop body executes. If \coqdocvar{P} contradicts \coqdocvar{b}, this
    holds trivially since the precondition is false.


    For instance, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} = 0 is a loop invariant of


      while X = 2 do X := 1 end


    since the program will never enter the loop. 

 The program


    while Y > 10 do Y := Y - 1; Z := Z + 1 end


    admits an interesting loop invariant:


    X = Y + Z


    Note that this doesn't contradict the loop guard but neither
    is it a command invariant of


    Y := Y - 1; Z := Z + 1


    since, if X = 5,
    Y = 0 and Z = 5, running the command will set Y + Z to 6. The
    loop guard \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} > 10 guarantees that this will not be the case.
    We will see many such loop invariants in the following chapter.

\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{-}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{simpl}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{intros}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{st}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{[}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Hinv}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Hguard}}\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{]}}. \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{congruence}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Qed}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Of course, this result is not surprising if we remember that
    the definition of \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.valid hoare triple}{\coqdocdefinition{valid\_hoare\_triple}} asserts that the postcondition
    must hold \textit{only} when the command terminates.  If the command
    doesn't terminate, we can prove anything we like about the
    post-condition.


    Hoare rules that specify what happens \textit{if} commands terminate,
    without proving that they do, are said to describe a logic of
    \textit{partial} correctness.  It is also possible to give Hoare rules
    for \textit{total} correctness, which additionally specifies that
    commands must terminate. Total correctness is out of the scope of
    this textbook. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsubsection{Exercise: \texorpdfstring{\protect}{REPEAT}\coqdocvar{REPEAT}}



\paragraph{Exercise: 4 stars, advanced, optional (hoare\_repeat)}




    In this exercise, we'll add a new command to our language of
    commands: \coqdocvar{REPEAT} c \coqdoctac{until} b \coqdockw{end}. You will write the
    evaluation rule for \coqdocvar{REPEAT} and add a new Hoare rule to the
    language for programs involving it.  (You may recall that the
    evaluation rule is given in an example in the \coqdocvar{Auto} chapter.
    Try to figure it out yourself here rather than peeking.)

\coqdocvar{REPEAT} behaves like \coqdocvar{while}, except that the loop guard is
    checked \textit{after} each execution of the body, with the loop
    repeating as long as the guard stays \textit{false}.  Because of this,
    the body will always execute at least once.

  \footnote{\url{ X > 0 }}
    repeat
      Y := X;
      X := X - 1
    until X = 0 end
  \footnote{\url{ X = 0 /\ Y > 0 }}

\section{Summary}



 So far, we've introduced Hoare Logic as a tool for reasoning about
    Imp programs.


    The rules of Hoare Logic are:
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (hoare\_asgn)
             \footnote{\url{Q [X |-> a]}} X:=a \footnote{\url{Q}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}  (hoare\_skip)
             \footnote{\url{ P }} skip \footnote{\url{ P }}


               \footnote{\url{ P }} c1 \footnote{\url{ Q }}
               \footnote{\url{ Q }} c2 \footnote{\url{ R }}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}  (hoare\_seq)
              \footnote{\url{ P }} c1;c2 \footnote{\url{ R }}


              \footnote{\url{P /\   b}} c1 \footnote{\url{Q}}
              \footnote{\url{P /\ ~ b}} c2 \footnote{\url{Q}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}  (hoare\_if)
      \footnote{\url{P}} if b then c1 else c2 end \footnote{\url{Q}}


               \footnote{\url{P /\ b}} c \footnote{\url{P}}
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}  (hoare\_while)
        \footnote{\url{P}} while b do c end \footnote{\url{P /\ ~ b}}


                \footnote{\url{P'}} c \footnote{\url{Q'}}
                   P ->> P'
                   Q' ->> Q
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (hoare\_consequence)
                \footnote{\url{P}} c \footnote{\url{Q}}


 Our main task in this chapter has been to \textit{define} the rules of
    Hoare logic, and prove that the definitions are sound.  Having
    done so, we can go on and work \textit{within} Hoare logic to prove that
    particular programs satisfy particular Hoare triples.  In the next
    chapter, we'll see how Hoare logic is can be used to prove that
    more interesting programs satisfy interesting specifications of
    their behavior.


    Crucially, we will do so without ever again \coqdoctac{unfold}ing the
    definition of Hoare triples -- i.e., we will take the rules of
    Hoare logic as a closed world for reasoning about programs. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}

\subsection{Havoc}



 In this exercise, we will derive proof rules for a \coqdocvar{HAVOC}
    command, which is similar to the nondeterministic \coqdocvar{any} expression
    from the the \coqdoclibrary{Imp} chapter.


    First, we enclose this work in a separate module, and recall the
    syntax and big-step semantics of Himp commands.

\coqdocindent{1.00em}

\subsection{Assert and Assume}



\paragraph{Exercise: 4 stars, standard (assert\_vs\_assume)}




    In this exercise, we will extend IMP with two commands, \coqdoctac{assert}
    and \coqdocvar{assume}. Both commands are ways to indicate that a certain
    assertion should hold any time this part of the program is
    reached. However they differ as follows:



\begin{itemize}
\item  If an \coqdoctac{assert} statement fails, it causes the program to go into
      an error state and exit.



\item  If an \coqdocvar{assume} statement fails, the program fails to evaluate at
      all. In other words, the program gets stuck and has no final
      state.

\end{itemize}


    The new set of commands is:

To define the behavior of \coqdoctac{assert} and \coqdocvar{assume}, we need to add
    notation for an error, which indicates that an assertion has
    failed. We modify the \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.ceval}{\coqdocinductive{ceval}} relation, therefore, so that
    it relates a start state to either an end state or to \coqdocvar{error}.
    The \coqref{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.result}{\coqdocinductive{result}} type indicates the end value of a program,
    either a state or an error: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Inductive} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.result}{result}{\coqdocinductive{result}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{Type}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:=}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{|}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.RNormal}{RNormal}{\coqdocconstructor{RNormal}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Imp.state}{\coqdocdefinition{state}} \coqexternalref{::type scope:x '->' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Logic}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\rightarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.result:252}{\coqdocinductive{result}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{|}}} \coqdef{SECF.Hoare.HoareAssertAssume.RError}{RError}{\coqdocconstructor{RError}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.:assn:assertion scope:x '[' x '|->' x ']'}{\coqdocnotation{:}} \coqref{SECF.Hoare.result:252}{\coqdocinductive{result}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}

We redefine hoare triples: Now, \{\{\coqdocvar{P}\}\} \coqdocvar{c} \{\{\coqdocvar{Q}\}\} means that,
    whenever \coqdocvar{c} is started in a state satisfying \coqdocvar{P}, and terminates
    with result \coqdocvar{r}, then \coqdocvar{r} is not an error and the state of \coqdocvar{r}
    satisfies \coqdocvar{Q}.

To test your understanding of this modification, give an example
    precondition and postcondition that are satisfied by the \coqdocvar{assume}
    statement but not by the \coqdoctac{assert} statement.

    semantics.  You don't need to do anything with these.
